(1,\frac{4}{3},\frac{4}{3})

➄
|J_{S}|,U\gg\!T_{\rm K};\,~J_{S}\!=\!J_{D}\!<0

T

(-\frac{1}{3},-\frac{4}{3},-\frac{4}{3})

Table 1:

The renormalized parameters in certain limits. The atomic GS represents the two-particle atomic ground states of the SVAIM in the zero-hybridization limit. In our definition
T_{\rm K}=\widetilde{\Delta}_{0}
in the FL phase. The numerical labels correspond to those indicated in
fig.

3
(e)–(g), marking where each relation holds.

Figure 4:
T_{\rm K}
near the critical points. The blue dots mark
T_{\rm K}
as a function of
\left(J^{(c)}_{D}\!-\!J_{D}\right)^{-1/2}
when
J_{S}\!=\!0
in (a), and as a function of
J^{(c)}_{S}\!-\!J_{S}
when
J_{D}\!=\!0.05
in (b). The orange solid lines in (a) and (b) are BKT-type and quadratic fitting curves, respectively.

The behavior of
T_{\rm K}
near critical points clarifies the nature of the phase transitions. We plot
T_{\rm K}
as a function of
J_{D}
with
J_{S}\!=\!0
near the FL-to-AD transition in
fig.

4
(a), and as a function of
J_{S}
with
J_{D}\!=\!0.05
near the FL-to-LS transition in
fig.

4
(b). The critical value
J^{(c)}_{D}\!\approx\!0.137
for
J_{S}\!=\!0
and
J^{(c)}_{S}\approx 0.08026
for
J_{D}\!=\!0.05
. We find that

•

near the FL-to-AD critical point, the Kondo temperature can be fitted with a BKT form
T_{\rm K}\propto e^{-c\sqrt{\frac{\Delta_{0}}{J^{(c)}_{D}-J_{D}}}}
for some constant
c
, consistent with our analytical RG calculation and previous numerical results
Galpin et al. 2005
. In
section

I.2
of SM
sup
, we further show that from the finite-size NRG spectra, we can numerically obtain how
\lambda_{x},\lambda_{z}
flow with the energy scale as plotted in
fig.

11
(d), validating the analytical RG equation
eqs.

7
and
8
.

•

near the FL-to-LS critical point, the Kondo temperature can be fitted by a quadratic function
T_{\rm K}\propto(J^{(c)}_{S}-J_{S})^{2}
, consistent with our analytical RG calculation and previous numerical results
Fabrizio et al. 2003
;
Leo and Fabrizio 2004
;
Nishikawa et al. 2012
.

Therefore, in the SVAIM, the FL-to-AD transition is BKT-type, and the FL-to-LS transition is second-order.

Appendix A
Bosonization-refermionization dictionary

To apply the bosonization technique to the impurity problem, we linearize the dispersion of bath electrons near the Fermi surface, extend the band width to infinity, and only keep the
s
-wave bath states that interact with the impurity. This effectively reduces the bath to 1-dimensional chiral fermions.

For the bosonization identities, we follow the constructive approach in Refs.
von Delft and Schoeller 1998
;
von Delft et al. 1998
;
Zaránd and von
Delft 2000
.
We will treat the Klein factors that carry the quantum numbers in an exact manner, which helps keep track of the physical states in the enlarged Hilbert space.
We also keep the
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
terms to analyze the finite-size spectrum, where
L
denotes the bath system size.
But we will ignore them when calculating physical quantities in the thermodynamic limit, such as the partition functions and correlation functions.

For all the models studied in this work, the Fermi velocities of all bath flavors will be dictated by symmetries to be degenerate, hence we set them as
v_{F}=1
.
We also set the Planck constant
\hbar=1
, elementary charge
|e|=1
, and the Boltzmann constant
k_{B}=1
, so all physical quantities can be measured in terms of the energy dimension.

A.1
Operator identity

Let
\alpha
label the flavor of bath electrons.
The chiral fermions can be formally put on a circle of length
L
, hence the finite-size energy spacing between two adjacent single-electron levels is
\frac{2\pi}{L}
.
The Hamiltonian reads,

\displaystyle H_{0}=\sum_{k}\sum_{\alpha}k:d^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(k)d_{\alpha}(k):\qquad\qquad k\in\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(\mathbb{Z}-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2}\right)

(9)

with
\left\{d^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(k),d_{\alpha}(k^{\prime})\right\}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
,
\left\{d_{\alpha}(k),d_{\alpha}(k^{\prime})\right\}=0
.
Here,
P_{\rm bc}=0,1
indicates whether the chemical potential lies exactly within a single-electron level, or between two levels.
The normal-ordering of chiral fermions
:\cdots:
is defined with respect to the following background
|0\rangle_{0}
,

\displaystyle d_{\alpha}(k)|0\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=0\qquad(\textrm{if}~~k>0)\qquad\qquad\qquad d^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(k)|0\rangle_{0}=0\qquad(\textrm{if}~~k\leq 0)\ .

(10)

Note that
|0\rangle_{0}
occupies all
non-positive
levels including zero.
The Fourier transformation to real-space reads

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{L}}\sum_{k}d_{\alpha}(k)~e^{-\mathrm{i}kx},\qquad\qquad d_{\alpha}(k)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{L}}\int_{-\frac{L}{2}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\mathrm{d}x~\psi_{\alpha}(x)~e^{\mathrm{i}kx}

(11)

with
\{\psi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x),\psi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\}=\delta(x-x^{\prime})\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
,
\{\psi_{\alpha}(x),\psi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\}=0
.
Since
P_{\rm bc}=0,1
also determines whether the boundary condition at
x=\pm\frac{L}{2}
is periodic or anti-periodic, we term it as the boundary condition parameter.
In the real-space, the Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}x\sum_{\alpha}:\psi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x)\left(\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}\right)\psi_{\alpha}(x):

(12)

The U(1) charge that counts the total particle number in each flavor
\alpha
is defined as

\displaystyle N_{\alpha}=\sum_{k}:d^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(k)d_{\alpha}(k):~=\int\mathrm{d}x:\psi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x):~\in\mathbb{Z}

(13)

Note that the chiral fermions are all
left-movers
, and, to keep a consistent notation with Ref.
von Delft and Schoeller 1998
, we have adopted the convention
\psi_{\alpha}(x)\sim d_{\alpha}(k)e^{-\mathrm{i}kx}
such that
d_{\alpha}(k)
is an eigenmode of the energy
k
.
(If the more common convention
\psi_{\alpha}(x)\sim d_{\alpha}(k)e^{\mathrm{i}kx}
were adopted,
d_{\alpha}(k)
would have an eigenenergy of
-k
.)

Bosonization relies on the fact that, any
\vec{N}
-particle Fock state in the physical Hilbert space, where
\vec{N}
collects all quantum numbers
N_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}
into a vector, can be constructed from a unique
\vec{N}
-particle ground state
|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}
by acting upon it a series of particle-hole excitations that commute with
\vec{N}
.
All operators within the physical Hilbert space can thus be constructed from two types of elements:
1) the Klein factors
F_{\alpha}
that link between
\vec{N}
-particle
ground
states
|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}
with different
\vec{N}
, and encode the fermion anti-commutation between different
\alpha
, and 2) the bosonic fields
\phi_{\alpha}(x)
that generate density fluctuations (i.e. particle-hole excitations) that commute with all
N_{\alpha^{\prime}}
.
Also,
[F_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x)]=0
.
We refer the detailed derivation of the bosonization procedures to Ref.
von Delft and Schoeller 1998
, and only summarize the definitions and key identities below.

The fermion operator is bosonized to,

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{F_{\alpha}}{\sqrt{2\pi x_{c}}}~e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{\alpha}(x)}~e^{-\mathrm{i}\left(N_{\alpha}-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2}\right)\frac{2\pi x}{L}}\ .

(14)

Here,
x_{c}\to 0^{+}
is an ultraviolet cutoff.
We remark that the fermion Hilbert space (as well as the boson Hilbert space introduced below) is not truncated, and
x_{c}\to 0^{+}
is only introduced to realize the operator identity.
Sometimes
x_{c}^{-1}
can be interpreted as an “effective bandwidth” of the chiral fermion.

F_{\alpha}
are Klein factors that obey

\displaystyle[N_{\alpha},F_{\alpha^{\prime}}]=-F_{\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\ ,\qquad F_{\alpha}F^{\dagger}_{\alpha}=F^{\dagger}_{\alpha}F_{\alpha}=1\ ,\qquad\{F_{\alpha},F^{\dagger}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\}=2\cdot\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\ ,\qquad\{F_{\alpha},F_{\alpha^{\prime}}\}=2F^{2}_{\alpha}\cdot\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\ .

(15)

After specifying a certain ordering of the fermion flavors
\alpha=1,2,\cdots
, we can define the normalized
\vec{N}
-particle ground states as

|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}=(F_{1}^{\dagger})^{N_{1}}(F_{2}^{\dagger})^{N_{2}}\cdots|0\rangle_{0}\ .

(16)

where we take the convention that
(F_{\alpha}^{\dagger})^{N_{\alpha}}=(F_{\alpha})^{-N_{\alpha}}
if
N_{\alpha}<0
.
Correspondingly, the matrix elements of Klein factors under the basis set
|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}
read

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}=(-1)^{\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha}N_{\alpha^{\prime}}}|\vec{N}-\Delta\vec{N}_{\alpha}\rangle_{0}\qquad\qquad\Delta\vec{N}_{\alpha}=(0,\cdots,\underset{\alpha\mathrm{-th}}{1},\cdots,0)

(17)

where
(-1)^{\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha}N_{\alpha^{\prime}}}
is the Jordan-Wigner string due to the anti-commutation between Klein factors.
It suffices to specify the action of
F_{\alpha}
on the
\vec{N}
-particle ground states, because all the bosonic operators that generate particle-hole excitations commute with
F_{\alpha}
.

The bosonic field
\phi_{\alpha}(x)
is defined as

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}-\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}\Big(e^{-\mathrm{i}qx}b_{\alpha}(q)+e^{\mathrm{i}qx}b^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(q)\Big)e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}=\varphi_{\alpha}(x)+\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)

(18)

\displaystyle b^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}\sum_{k}d^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(k+q)d_{\alpha}(k)\ ,\qquad\qquad q\in\frac{2\pi}{L}\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{\frac{2\pi}{L},2\frac{2\pi}{L},3\frac{2\pi}{L},\cdots\}

(19)

By this definition,
\phi_{\alpha}(x)
is always periodic under
x\to x+L
.
Boson fields obey
\big[b_{\alpha}(q),b^{\dagger}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})\big]=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{qq^{\prime}}
,
\big[b_{\alpha}(q),b_{\alpha^{\prime}}(q^{\prime})\big]=0
, and
[N_{\alpha^{\prime}},b_{\alpha}(q)]=0
.
We have also separately defined
\varphi_{\alpha}(x)
and
\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)
, which are the components of
\phi_{\alpha}(x)
that only consist of boson annihilation and creation operators, respectively.

We first compute the commutator

[\varphi_{\alpha}(x),\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}e^{(-\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{L}(x-x^{\prime})-\frac{2\pi}{L}x_{c})n}=-\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\ln\left(1-e^{-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{L}(x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c})}\right)\ ,

(20)

where
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}y^{n}=-\ln(1-y)
is used for
|y|<1
.
Hence

[\phi_{\alpha}(x),\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]=-\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\ln\frac{1-e^{-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{L}(x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c})}}{1-e^{-\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{L}(x^{\prime}-x-\mathrm{i}x_{c})}}=-2\mathrm{i}\cdot\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot\arg\left(1-e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{L}(x-x^{\prime})}e^{-\frac{2\pi}{L}x_{c}}\right)\ .

(21)

Here, the single-valued branch of the above
\ln\frac{\cdots}{\cdots}
function is always taken such that the commutator equals
0
if
x=x^{\prime}
mod
L
, so that the
\arg(\cdots)
function takes values in
(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
.
Taylor-expanding
eqs.

20
and
21
with regard to
\frac{x-x^{\prime}}{L}
yields, respectively,

\displaystyle[\varphi_{\alpha}(x),\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\left[-\ln\left(\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{L}\big(x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c}\big)\right)+\frac{\pi\mathrm{i}}{L}(x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c})\right]+\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})\ .

(22)

\displaystyle[\phi_{\alpha}(x),\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\bigg[\ln\left(\frac{x^{\prime}-x-\mathrm{i}x_{c}}{x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c}}\right)+\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}}{L}(x-x^{\prime})\bigg]+\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})\ .

(23)

Notice that while
eqs.

20
and
21
are periodic in
x\to x+L
and
x^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}+L
, the taylor-expanded
eqs.

22
and
23
are not.
Hereafter we will omit
\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})
terms in the real-space commutators, unless otherwise specified.

We write the commutator of the
\phi
fields in a more commonly used form,

\displaystyle[\phi_{\alpha}(x),\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot(-\pi\mathrm{i})\cdot\left(\mathrm{sgn}_{x_{c}}(x-x^{\prime})-\frac{2}{L}(x-x^{\prime})\right),\qquad\mathrm{sgn}_{x_{c}}(x)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arctan\frac{x}{x_{c}}

(24)

\displaystyle[\phi_{\alpha}(x),\partial_{x^{\prime}}\phi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]=\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\cdot(2\pi\mathrm{i})\cdot\left(\delta_{x_{c}}(x-x^{\prime})-\frac{1}{L}\right),\qquad\delta_{x_{c}}(x)=\frac{x_{c}}{\pi}\frac{1}{x^{2}+x_{c}^{2}}\ ,

(25)

where the single-branch of
\frac{1}{2\mathrm{i}}\ln\frac{\mathrm{i}-z}{\mathrm{i}+z}=\arctan(z)
is taken in such a way that
\arctan(0)=0
.

Anti-commutation between fermion operators (
eq.

14
) with different flavors is guaranteed by the Klein factors.
We now verify the anti-commutation between fermion operators within the same flavor.
First,

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi x_{c}}F_{\alpha}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}x}\cdot F_{\alpha}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}x^{\prime}}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{\alpha}(x)}e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi x_{c}}F_{\alpha}^{2}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}(x+x^{\prime})}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{\alpha}(x)}e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})}

(26)

where we have made use of
F_{\alpha}^{\dagger}N_{\alpha}F_{\alpha}=N_{\alpha}-1
.
By the Baker-Hausdorff formula

e^{A}e^{B}=e^{A+B+\frac{1}{2}[A,B]},\qquad(\text{provided }[A,[A,B]]=[B,[A,B]]=0)

(27)

and
eq.

24
, we have

\psi_{\alpha}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2\pi x_{c}}F_{\alpha}^{2}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}(x+x^{\prime})}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{L}(x+x^{\prime})}\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}(\phi_{\alpha}(x)+\phi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime}))}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}\mathrm{sgn}_{x_{c}}(x-x^{\prime})}\ .

(28)

Notice that the second term in the
[\phi_{\alpha}(x),\phi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})]
commutator (
eq.

24
) changes the phase factor
e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{L}x}
to
e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{L}(x+x^{\prime})}
.
Given
x_{c}\to 0^{+}
, the above result immediately leads to
\{\psi_{\alpha}(x),\psi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})\}=0
.
One can similarly verify
\{\psi_{\alpha}(x),\psi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x^{\prime})\}=0
for
x\neq x^{\prime}
.

We then consider the operator
\psi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})
in the
x^{\prime}\to x
limit.
To simplify the calculation, we first rewrite the fermion operator in a normal ordered (with respect to boson vacuum) form

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{F_{\alpha}}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}x}e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)}e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)}=\frac{F_{\alpha}}{\sqrt{L}}e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}x}:e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi_{\alpha}(x)}:

(29)

where we have made use of

e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)}=e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)}e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)}e^{\frac{1}{2}[\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x),\varphi_{\alpha}(x)]}

and
[\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x),\varphi_{\alpha}(x)]=\ln\frac{2\pi x_{c}}{L}
.
Here
:\cdots:
represents normal ordering with respect to the boson vacuum.
Then we have

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}e^{\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}x}F_{\alpha}^{\dagger}F_{\alpha}e^{-\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}x^{\prime}}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x)}e^{\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)}e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})}e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L}e^{\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}(x-x^{\prime})}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x)}\left[e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})}e^{\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x)}\cdot e^{[\varphi_{\alpha}(x),\varphi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x^{\prime})]}\right]e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c}}e^{\mathrm{i}(N_{\alpha}-P_{\rm bc}/2+1/2)\frac{2\pi}{L}(x-x^{\prime})}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}(\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x)-\varphi^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(x^{\prime}))}e^{\mathrm{i}(\varphi_{\alpha}(x)-\varphi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime}))}\ .

(30)

Taking
x_{c}\to 0^{+}
first and then Taylor-expanding
x-x^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c}}+\frac{N_{\alpha}+1/2-P_{\rm bc}/2}{L}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}(x)+\frac{x-x^{\prime}}{4\pi}\left(\mathrm{i}:(\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}(x))^{2}:+\partial_{x}^{2}\phi_{\alpha}(x)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{i}(x-x^{\prime})\frac{N_{\alpha}+1/2-P_{\rm bc}/2}{L}\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}(x)+\mathcal{O}((x-x^{\prime})^{2})\ .

(31)

Recall that
\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})
terms are also omitted.
The normal ordered density operator, where the constant term
\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{1}{x-x^{\prime}-\mathrm{i}x_{c}}+\frac{1/2-P_{\rm bc}/2}{L}
is removed, is then given by

:\psi_{\alpha}^{\dagger}(x)\psi_{\alpha}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}(x)+\frac{N_{\alpha}}{L}\ ,

(32)

which is consistent with
eq.

18
.

We substitute
section

A.1
into the kinetic energy Hamiltonian
eq.

12
and obtain

H_{0}=\sum_{\alpha}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{4\pi}\left(:(\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}(x))^{2}:+\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})\right)\ .

(33)

Integral over full derivative terms,
e.g.,

\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}
and
\partial_{x}^{2}\phi_{\alpha}
, vanishes due to the periodic boundary condition of the boson field.
The omitted
\mathcal{O}(L^{-2})
term in the integrand will contribute to an
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
term to the total energy, which is of interest.
To obtain this term, we consider the vacuum
|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}
defined in
eq.

16
.
Since the operator
:(\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha})^{2}:
kills
|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}
, the
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
term determines the energy of
|\vec{N}\rangle_{0}
, which can be simply counted as

\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{n=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\frac{2\pi}{L}(n-P_{\rm bc}/2)=\frac{2\pi}{L}\frac{N_{\alpha}(N_{\alpha}+1-P_{\rm bc})}{2}
.
Therefore, we conclude that the kinetic energy Hamiltonian is

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{4\pi}:\big(\partial_{x}\phi_{\alpha}(x)\big)^{2}:+\sum_{\alpha}\frac{2\pi}{L}\frac{N_{\alpha}(N_{\alpha}+1-P_{\rm bc})}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{q>0}q:b^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(q)b_{\alpha}(q):+\sum_{\alpha}\frac{2\pi}{L}\frac{N_{\alpha}(N_{\alpha}+1-P_{\rm bc})}{2}\ .

(34)

Note that the
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
term relies on the definition of
|0\rangle_{0}
, which is chosen to occupy all non-positive levels including zero.

We remark again that
eqs.

14
,
25
,
24
,
A.1
and
32
contain
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
terms.
We will keep these
\mathcal{O}(L^{-1})
terms when discussing the finite-size spectrum and neglect them otherwise.

A.2
Phase shift due to
\delta
-potential

Following Refs.
Andrei et al. 1983
;
von Delft et al. 1998
;
Krishnan and Metlitski 2024
, we discuss and compute the phase shift
\pi\rho
generated by a
\delta
-function potential of strength
\lambda\cdot 2\pi
.
For this purpose, it suffices to consider a single-flavor problem, and drop the flavor index
\alpha
in this subsection.

Phase shift

The second-quantized Hamiltonian reads,

\displaystyle H_{0}+H_{1}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}x:\psi^{\dagger}(x)(\mathrm{i}\partial_{x})\psi(x):+~\lambda\cdot(2\pi):\psi^{\dagger}(0)\psi(0):

(35)

which in the first-quantized language corresponds to an eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}+\lambda\cdot 2\pi\cdot\delta(0)\Big)\psi(x)

\displaystyle=k\cdot\psi(x)

(36)

It can be solved by the following ansatz (with the normalization factor ignored),

\displaystyle\psi(x)\sim e^{-\mathrm{i}kx}e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\rho}\qquad(\mathrm{if}~~x<0)\qquad\qquad\psi(x)\sim e^{-\mathrm{i}kx}e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\rho}\qquad(\mathrm{if}~~x>0)

(37)

with the phase shift
\pi\rho
to be determined.
But the relation between
\rho
and
\lambda
depends on how one regularizes the delta potential at the high-energy end.

Following the discussions in Ref.
von Delft et al. 1998
, we regularize the delta potential as

\displaystyle H_{1}=(\lambda\cdot 2\pi)\int\mathrm{d}x~\delta_{x_{c}}(x)\int\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}~\delta_{x_{c}}(x^{\prime}):\psi^{\dagger}(x)\psi(x^{\prime}):

(38)

x_{c}
restricts us to such processes where the momenta
k^{\prime}
and
k
of both the incoming and outgoing electrons are individually within a cutoff
\mathcal{O}({x_{c}}^{-1})
, which can be understood as a zero-range potential in a finite-width band.
If one were to choose another regularization
H_{1}=(\lambda\cdot 2\pi)\int\mathrm{d}x~\delta_{y_{c}}(x):\psi^{\dagger}(x)\psi(x):
, which only dictates the momentum difference
k-k^{\prime}
to be within
\mathcal{O}({y_{c}}^{-1})
, and can be understood as a finite-range (
y_{c}
) potential in an infinite-width band (
y_{c}\gg x_{c}
), then the
\rho(\lambda)
relation would be different.
We will mainly focus on the first scheme.

Corresponding to the regularization scheme
eq.

38
, the first-quantized eigen-value problem is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}\psi(x)+(\lambda\cdot 2\pi)\cdot\delta_{x_{c}}(x)\int\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}~\delta_{x_{c}}(x^{\prime})~\psi(x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=k\cdot\psi(x)

(39)

For electrons far below the cutoff,
kx_{c}\to 0^{+}
, using the ansatz
eq.

37
,
\int\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}~\delta(x^{\prime})\psi(x^{\prime})=\frac{\psi(0^{-})+\psi(0^{+})}{2}
is an average of
\psi(0^{-})
and
\psi(0^{+})
.
Therefore, by further integrating
eq.

39
over an inifinitesimal region containing
x=0
, we can solve the phase shift

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\Big(\psi(0^{+})-\psi(0^{-})\Big)+(\lambda\cdot 2\pi)\frac{\psi(0^{+})+\psi(0^{-})}{2}=0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\rho=\frac{\arctan(\lambda\pi)}{\pi}\in\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)\ .

(40)

In the second regularization scheme, we can similarly derive an eigen-equation, i.e.,

\mathrm{i}\partial_{x}\psi(x)+(\lambda\cdot 2\pi)\delta_{y_{c}}(x)\psi(x)=k\cdot\psi(x)
, and obtain the phase shift
\rho=\lambda
.

In terms of the finite-size spectrum, the phase shift also manifests as a global shift of all single-electron levels.
Specifically, if one fixes
\psi(-\frac{L}{2})=e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi P_{\rm bc}}\cdot\psi(\frac{L}{2})
, then the momentum
k
in
eq.

37
, which is also the energy, must be quantized into

\displaystyle k\in\frac{2\pi}{L}\Big(\mathbb{Z}-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2}+\rho\Big)

(41)

Therefore, if one gradually turns on
\lambda
, all the electron levels, which are equally spaced by
\frac{2\pi}{L}
, will be shifted upward together by an amount of
\rho\cdot\frac{2\pi}{L}
.
The maximal shift is equal to
half
of the level spacing, and is only achieved when
\lambda\to\infty
.

Due to our regularization scheme
eq.

38
, we should not bosonize
H_{1}
by directly applying the point-splitting in
sections

A.1
and
32
, because the latter relies on the order
\lim_{x^{\prime}\to x}\lim_{x_{c}\to 0^{+}}
of taking limits, whereas
eq.

38
has
|x-x^{\prime}|\sim x_{c}
.
Nevertheless, we can still formally write

\displaystyle H_{1}=\lambda^{\prime}\int\mathrm{d}x~\delta(x)\partial_{x}\phi(x)+\lambda^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\pi}{L}N=\lambda^{\prime}~\partial_{x}\phi(x)\Big|_{x=0}+\lambda^{\prime\prime}\frac{2\pi}{L}N\ ,

(42)

where
N
counts the fermion number.

\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}
can be directly determined by the phase shift
\rho
at large distances.
Importantly, this determination does not depend on the regularization of the
\delta
-potential, which further relates
\rho
to the potential
\lambda
.
Suppose we were using the second regularization scheme, where the point-splitting in
sections

A.1
and
32
applies, then there would be
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\rho
with
\rho=\lambda
being the phase shift.
As the relation between
\lambda^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime\prime}
and
\rho
should not depend on the regularization,
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\rho
must also hold for the first regularization scheme except that now
\rho
is given by
eq.

40
.
One can verify this statement by examining the phase shift and finit-size spectrum.
First, viewing
\phi
as a classical field, the
\delta
-potential generates a kink
\phi(0^{+})-\phi(0^{-})=-2\pi\lambda^{\prime}
in its solution, corresponding to a phase shift
e^{\mathrm{i}2\pi\lambda^{\prime}}
in the fermion field, confirming
\lambda^{\prime}=\rho
is the phase shift.
Second, according to the discussion above
section

A.1
, the finite-size ground-state energy with a phase shift
\rho
is

\frac{2\pi}{L}\frac{N(N+1+2\rho-P_{\rm bc})}{2}
, which is changed by
\frac{2\pi}{L}N\rho
compared to the un-shifted spectrum.
This confirms
\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\rho
.
Therefore, in the first regularization scheme there must be

\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\rho=\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan(\lambda\pi)\ .

(43)

Readers may refer to Ref.
Kotliar and Si 1996
;
Schiller and De Leo 2008
) for further discussions.

Gauge transformation canceling the
\delta
-potential

Due to the above discussions,
H_{0}+H_{1}
can be readily diagonalized in the original fermion representation, with eigenstates
|G\rangle
given by the phase-shifted fermion spectrum.
Now we show that, one can also apply a gauge transformation
U=e^{\mathrm{i}\rho\phi(0)}
, where
\phi(0)
is the bosonized field (see
eq.

14
), so that
\overline{H}=UHU^{\dagger}
reduces to a free Hamiltonian without phase shift.
The eigenstates of
H
in the original representation will thus be given by
|G\rangle=U^{\dagger}|\overline{G}\rangle
, where
|\overline{G}\rangle
is the eigenstate of the free-fermion (free-boson) Hamiltonian
\overline{H}
.

By applying the formula

e^{A}\cdot B\cdot e^{-A}=B+[A,B]+\frac{1}{2!}[A,[A,B]]+\cdots

(44)

and
eq.

24
, we obtain

\displaystyle U~\phi(x)~U^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\phi(x)-\pi\rho\cdot\mathrm{sgn}_{x_{c}}(x)+\frac{2\pi\rho}{L}x

(45)

\displaystyle U~\psi(x)~U^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\psi(x)~e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\rho\cdot\mathrm{sgn}_{x_{c}}(x)}~e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi\rho}{L}x}

(46)

\displaystyle U~\partial_{x}\phi(x)~U^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\phi(x)-2\pi\rho\cdot\delta_{x_{c}}(x)+\frac{2\pi\rho}{L}

(47)

Applying this gauge transformation to the kinetic energy term
\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\mathrm{d}x:(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}:
generates a
\delta
-potential term of the form
-\rho\int\mathrm{d}x~\delta_{x_{c}}(x)\partial_{x}\phi(x)
, which can be used to cancel the coupling Hamiltonian
H_{1}
.
Thus, we expect that
U(H_{0}+H_{1})U^{\dagger}
is a free theory.

However, as the finite-size spectrum is of concern, one cannot naively apply the gauge transformation (
eq.

47
) in real space, which, as well as the commutator
eq.

24
, are derived by taking the
L\to\infty
limit before
x_{c}\to 0^{+}
.
As explained in Ref.
von Delft and Schoeller 1998
(see discussions around its Eq. (45)), the two limits do not commute.
To obtain the
exact
form of
U(H_{0}+H_{1})U^{\dagger}
, we work in momentum space and keep its
exact
dependencies on
x_{c}
and
L
until the end.
Since

\phi(0)=\sum_{q>0}-\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}(b(q)+b^{\dagger}(q))e^{-x_{c}q/2}\ ,\qquad[\phi(0),b(q)]=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}e^{-x_{c}q/2}\ ,

(48)

eq.

44
implies the
exact
transformation

\displaystyle U~b(q)~U^{\dagger}=b(q)+\mathrm{i}\rho\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}\ .

(49)

It follows

\displaystyle U\left(\sum_{q}q~b^{\dagger}(q)b(q)\right)U^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q}q\left(b^{\dagger}(q)-\mathrm{i}\rho\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}\right)\left(b(q)+\mathrm{i}\rho\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}\right)

(50)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}q~b^{\dagger}(q)b(q)+\left(\rho\sum_{q>0}\mathrm{i}q\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}b^{\dagger}(q)e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}+\mathrm{H.c.}\right)+\frac{2\pi}{L}\sum_{q>0}\rho^{2}e^{-x_{c}q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}q~b^{\dagger}(q)b(q)-\rho~\partial_{x}\phi(x)\Big|_{x=0}+\frac{2\pi}{L}\rho^{2}\frac{e^{-x_{c}\frac{2\pi}{L}}}{1-e^{-x_{c}\frac{2\pi}{L}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}q~b^{\dagger}(q)b(q)-\rho~\partial_{x}\phi(x)\Big|_{x=0}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{x_{c}}\left(1-\frac{\pi}{L}x_{c}\right)+\frac{2\pi}{L}\mathcal{O}(x_{c}L^{-1})\ .

Since
U
commutes with the electron number operators
N
, for
H_{0}
in
section

A.1
(with
\alpha=1
), we have

\displaystyle UH_{0}U^{\dagger}=H_{0}-\rho~\partial_{x}\phi(x)\Big|_{x=0}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{x_{c}}\left(1-\frac{\pi}{L}x_{c}\right)+\mathcal{O}(x_{c}L^{-2})

(51)

Let
H_{1}=\rho~\partial_{x}\phi(x)\big|_{x=0}+\rho\frac{2\pi}{L}N
(
eq.

42
).
Then, since

[\partial_{x}\phi(x)\big|_{x=0},\phi(0)]=\sum_{q>0}\frac{2\pi}{qL}\left[-\mathrm{i}qb(q)+\mathrm{i}qb^{+}(q),b(q)+b^{\dagger}(q)\right]e^{-x_{c}q}=-2\mathrm{i}\frac{2\pi}{L}\frac{e^{-x_{c}\frac{2\pi}{L}}}{1-e^{-x_{c}\frac{2\pi}{L}}}=-2\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{x_{c}}\left(1-\frac{\pi}{L}x_{c}\right)+\mathcal{O}(x_{c}L^{-2})\ ,

(52)

eq.

44
leads to

\displaystyle UH_{1}U^{\dagger}=H_{1}-2\frac{\rho^{2}}{x_{c}}\left(1-\frac{\pi}{L}x_{c}\right)+\mathcal{O}(x_{c}L^{-2})

(53)

To conclude,

\displaystyle U(H_{0}+H_{1})U^{\dagger}=\sum_{q>0}q~b^{\dagger}(q)b(q)+\frac{2\pi}{L}\frac{N(N+1-P_{\rm bc})}{2}+\rho\frac{2\pi}{L}N-\frac{\rho^{2}}{x_{c}}\left(1-\frac{\pi}{L}x_{c}\right)+\mathcal{O}(x_{c}L^{-2})\ .

(54)

eq.

54
suggests that the ground state energy (for
N=0
) is changed by
\Delta E=-\frac{\rho^{2}}{x_{c}}\left(1-\frac{\pi}{L}x_{c}\right)
due to the
\delta
-potential.
The non-divergent energy change is
\frac{\pi}{L}\rho^{2}
.
We can reproduce this result from the fermion side using a much simpler argument.
Consider the potential
H_{1}=2\pi\rho\cdot\int\mathrm{d}x~\delta(x):\psi^{\dagger}(x)\psi(x):
with the second regularization such that it generates the correct phase shift
\rho\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
.
At the single-particle level,
\rho
shifts the level
k=\frac{2\pi}{L}(n-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2})
to
k=\frac{2\pi}{L}(n+\rho-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2})
, where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
For simplicity, here we assume
\rho
does not cause a level crossing, i.e.,
\rho<\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2}
.
To sum all the energy levels, we introduce an energy truncation factor
e^{-|k|\frac{L}{2\pi}\alpha}
(
\alpha\to 0^{+}
) for each level:

\displaystyle E(\rho)=\frac{2\pi}{L}\sum_{n\leq 0}\left(n+\rho-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2}\right)e^{(n+\rho-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2})\alpha}\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\to 0^{+}}}{{=}}\;\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}+\frac{1}{12}+\frac{\rho-P_{\rm bc}/2}{2}+\frac{(\rho-P_{\rm bc}/2)^{2}}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)

(55)

The energy change due to level-shift is
E(\rho)-E(0)=\frac{2\pi}{L}[\frac{\rho}{2}+\frac{\rho^{2}-P_{\rm bc}\rho}{2}]
.
Even
E_{1}(\rho)
and
E_{1}(0)
are individually divergent, the difference is finite.
After subtracting the constant

2\pi\rho\cdot\langle 0|\psi^{\dagger}(0)\psi(0)|0\rangle=\frac{2\pi}{L}\rho\sum_{n\leq 0}e^{(n-\frac{P_{\rm bc}}{2})\alpha}=\frac{2\pi}{L}\left(\frac{\rho}{\alpha}+\frac{\rho-\rho\cdot P_{\rm bc}}{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha)

(56)

due to the normal ordering in
H_{1}
, we obtain the total energy change

\Delta E=-\frac{2\pi}{L}\cdot\frac{\rho}{\alpha}+\frac{\pi}{L}\cdot\rho^{2}\ .

(57)

Its non-divergent part is the same as the exact result.

A.3
Correlation functions

For a free boson Hamiltonian
H_{0}
, the Green’s function of
\phi(x)
can be directly computed using the mode expansion
eq.

18
.
Specifically, we define the time-evolved (imaginary or real-time) boson fields by the free
H_{0}
as
\phi_{\alpha}(\tau,x)=e^{\tau H_{0}}\phi_{\alpha}(x)e^{-\tau H_{0}}
and
\phi_{\alpha}(t,x)=e^{\mathrm{i}tH_{0}}\phi_{\alpha}(x)e^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{0}}
, with expansion

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}(\tau,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}-\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}\Big(e^{-q(\mathrm{i}x+\tau)}b_{\alpha}(q)+e^{q(\mathrm{i}x+\tau)}b^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(q)\Big)e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}

(58)

\displaystyle\phi_{\alpha}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}-\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{qL}}\Big(e^{-\mathrm{i}q(x+t)}b_{\alpha}(q)+e^{\mathrm{i}q(x+t)}b^{\dagger}_{\alpha}(q)\Big)e^{-\frac{x_{c}q}{2}}

(59)

Because the bath electrons are left-movers, the time-evolved boson fields only depend on
\tau+\mathrm{i}x
and
\mathrm{i}(t+x)
. So will be the correlation functions.
It can then be calculated that, at zero temperature, for the free bosonic vacuum
|0\rangle
,

\displaystyle\Big\langle\phi(\tau,x)~\phi(0,0)\Big\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}\frac{2\pi}{qL}e^{-q(\mathrm{i}x+\tau+x_{c})}=^{1)}-\ln\left[1-e^{-\frac{2\pi}{L}(\tau+\mathrm{i}x+x_{c})}\right]=^{2)}-\ln\left[\frac{2\pi}{L}\big(\tau+\mathrm{i}x+x_{c}\big)\right]

(60)

\displaystyle\Big\langle\phi(0,0)~\phi(\tau,x)\Big\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q>0}\frac{2\pi}{qL}e^{q(\mathrm{i}x+\tau-x_{c})}=^{1)}-\ln\left[1-e^{-\frac{2\pi}{L}(-(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)+x_{c})}\right]=^{2)}-\ln\left[\frac{2\pi}{L}\big(-(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)+x_{c}\big)\right]

(61)

The same expressions apply to the real-time axis by replacing
\tau\to\mathrm{i}t
.
Several remarks are associated with the equal marks.
1) The series expansion
-\ln(1-x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{x^{n}}{n}
is convergent only for
-1\leq|x|<1
. For the correlation functions that we will consider in this work, namely, the
\tau
-ordered, the
t
-ordered, and the
t
-retarded correlation functions, the argument always meets the convergence criterion.
2) The thermodynamic limit
\frac{2\pi}{L}x,\frac{2\pi}{L}t,\frac{2\pi}{L}\tau\to 0
is taken and
\mathcal{O}{(L^{-2})}
terms are omitted, which will be our main focus in this paper for evaluating the correlation functions.

We tabulate the
\tau
-ordered,
t
-ordered correlation functions in below, with the divergence at
x,\tau,t\to 0
subtracted,

\displaystyle\Big\langle T_{\tau}~\phi(\tau,x)~\phi(0,0)\Big\rangle_{0}-\Big\langle\phi(0,0)^{2}\Big\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\ln\frac{x_{c}}{(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)+x_{c}}\qquad(T=0^{+})

(62)

\displaystyle\Big\langle T_{t}~\phi(t,x)~\phi(0,0)\Big\rangle_{0}-\Big\langle\phi(0,0)^{2}\Big\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\ln\frac{x_{c}}{\mathrm{i}(t+x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(t)+x_{c}}\qquad(T=0^{+})\ .

Following Ref.
von Delft and Schoeller 1998
(see its Eq. (74) and Appendix H2b), the finite-temperature imaginary time function can also be derived as

\Big\langle T_{\tau}~\phi(\tau,x)~\phi(0,0)\Big\rangle_{0}-\Big\langle\phi(0,0)^{2}\Big\rangle_{0}=\ln\frac{\sin(\pi Tx_{c})}{\sin\left[\pi T(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}\overset{x_{c}\to 0^{+}}{=}\ln\frac{\pi Tx_{c}}{\sin\left[\pi T(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}

(63)

where the limit
L\to\infty
is taken first.
It is periodic over the interval
\tau\in[-\frac{1}{2T},\frac{1}{2T}]
.
Since it reduces to the zero-temperature result in the
T\to 0^{+}
limit, the two orders of limits

\lim_{T\to 0^{+}}\lim_{x_{c}\to 0^{+}}\lim_{L\to\infty},\qquad\lim_{x_{c}\to 0^{+}}\lim_{L\to\infty}\lim_{T\to 0^{+}}

(64)

give the same correlation functions.

It is also useful to evaluate various correlation functions of the vertex operators
e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi}
. A two-point correlation can be calculated by exponentiating the boson correlation function, due to the following identity,

\displaystyle\left\langle e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(z_{2})}~e^{-\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(z_{1})}\right\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=e^{\kappa^{2}\big(\langle\phi(z_{2})~\phi(z_{1})\rangle_{0}-\left\langle\phi(0)^{2}\right\rangle_{0}\big)}

(65)

where
z_{2},z_{1}
can stand for any type of space-time arguments.
This identity can be proven in two steps.
First, using the Baker-Hausdorff formula (
eq.

27
), the left hand side equals to
\left\langle e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa(\phi(z_{2})-\phi(z_{1}))}\right\rangle_{0}\cdot e^{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}[\phi(z_{2}),\phi(z_{1})]}
.
Second, we use the identity

\left\langle e^{B}\right\rangle_{0}=\sum_{n=0,2,4\cdots}\frac{1}{(2n)!}\left\langle B^{2n}\right\rangle_{0}=e^{\frac{1}{2}\left\langle B^{2}\right\rangle_{0}}\ ,

(66)

where
B
is a linear superposition of boson creation and annihilation operators, and we have made use of Wick’s theorem
\langle B^{2n}\rangle_{0}=\frac{(2n)!}{2^{n}}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\langle B^{2}\rangle_{0}\right)^{n}
.
Then we have

\left\langle e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa(\phi(z_{2})-\phi(z_{1})}\right\rangle_{0}\cdot e^{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}[\phi(z_{2}),\phi(z_{1})]}=\exp\left(\kappa^{2}\left\langle\phi(z_{2})\phi(z_{1})-\phi^{2}(0)\right\rangle_{0}\right)
.

We tabulate some useful time-ordered correlation functions for future convenience:

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{\tau}~e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(\tau,x)}~e^{-\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(0,0)}\right\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\pi Tx_{c}}{\sin\left[\pi T(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}\overset{T\to 0^{+}}{=}\left(\frac{x_{c}}{(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)+x_{c}}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{t}~e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(t,x)}~e^{-\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(0,0)}\right\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{T=0^{+}}{=}\left(\frac{x_{c}}{\mathrm{i}(t+x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(t)+x_{c}}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}

\displaystyle\left\langle\left[e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(t,x)},e^{-\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi(0,0)}\right]\right\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle{=}\left(\frac{\pi Tx_{c}}{\sin\left[\mathrm{i}\pi T(t+x)+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}-\left(\frac{\pi Tx_{c}}{\sin\left[-\mathrm{i}\pi T(t+x)+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}

\displaystyle\overset{T\to 0^{+}}{=}\left(\frac{x_{c}}{\mathrm{i}(t+x)+x_{c}}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}-\left(\frac{x_{c}}{-\mathrm{i}(t+x)+x_{c}}\right)^{\kappa^{2}}

(67)

The notation
\overset{T\to 0^{+}}{=}
means that we take the
L\to\infty
limit first and then
T\to 0^{+}
, and the notation
\overset{T=0^{+}}{=}
means that we take
T\to 0^{+}
first and then
L\to\infty
, as specified in
eq.

64
.

\Delta=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}
is defined as the
scaling dimension
of the vertex operator
Q(t,x)=e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi}
, because upon the rescaling
t=bt^{\prime}
,
x=bx^{\prime}
,
Q(t,x)=b^{-\Delta}Q^{\prime}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime})
, the correlation function remains unchanged,
i.e.,

\left\langle Q(t,x)Q^{\dagger}(0,0)\right\rangle_{0}=b^{-2\Delta}\left\langle Q^{\prime}(t^{\prime},x^{\prime}),Q^{\prime\dagger}(0,0)\right\rangle_{0}
.
We dub
[e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa\phi}]=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}
,
[x]=[t]=-1
.

More generically, a
2n
-point correlator is given by

\displaystyle\left\langle e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa_{2n}\phi(z_{2n})}\cdots e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa_{i}\phi(z_{i})}\cdots e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa_{1}\phi(z_{1})}\right\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\sum_{i}\kappa_{i})^{2}\langle\phi(0)^{2}\rangle_{0}}\cdot e^{-\sum_{i^{\prime}>i}\kappa_{i^{\prime}}\kappa_{i}\left(\langle\phi(z_{i^{\prime}})\phi(z_{i})\rangle_{0}-\langle\phi(0)^{2}\rangle_{0}\right)}

(68)

Since
e^{-\langle\phi(0)^{2}\rangle_{0}}=\frac{2\pi x_{c}}{L}\to 0
, the first factor effectively dictates that the correlation function is non-zero only if
\sum_{j}\kappa_{j}=0
.
This is a manifestation of the effective
\rm U(1)
symmetry
\phi\to\phi+\mathrm{const}
in the free boson theory.
If we use the imaginary-time and specify the time-ordering as
\tau_{2n}>\cdots>\tau_{2}>\tau_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle T_{\tau}e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa_{2n}\phi(z_{2n})}\cdots e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa_{j}\phi(z_{j})}\cdots e^{\mathrm{i}\kappa_{1}\phi(z_{1})}\right\rangle_{0}

\displaystyle=\exp\left[-\sum_{i^{\prime}>i}\kappa_{i^{\prime}}\kappa_{i}\ln\left(\frac{\pi Tx_{c}}{\sin\left[\pi T(z_{i^{\prime}}-z_{i})+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{T\to 0^{+}}{=}\prod_{i^{\prime}>i}\exp\left[-\sum_{i^{\prime}>i}\kappa_{i^{\prime}}\kappa_{i}\ln\left(\frac{x_{c}}{z_{i^{\prime}}-z_{i}+x_{c}}\right)\right]\ ,

(69)

where
z_{i}=\tau_{i}+\mathrm{i}x_{i}
, provided
\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\kappa_{i}=0
.
For the same reason explained after
eq.

63
, the
\lim_{T\to 0^{+}}
and
\lim_{L\to\infty}
limits commute with each other for general vertex correlation functions.

section

A.3
will be useful in RG calculations and furnishes the Coulomb gas analog.

The free-fermion correlation function can also be recovered using
section

A.3
:

\displaystyle G(\tau,x)=

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)}{2\pi x_{c}}\left\langle T_{\tau}e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi(\tau,x)}e^{\mathrm{i}\phi(0,0)}\right\rangle_{0}{=}-\frac{\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)T}{2\sin\left[\pi T(\tau+\mathrm{i}x)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)+\pi Tx_{c}\right]}\overset{T\to 0^{+}}{=}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{\tau+\mathrm{i}x+x_{c}\mathrm{sgn}(\tau)}

\displaystyle G(t,x)\overset{T=0^{+}}{=}

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\left\langle T_{t}\psi(t,x)\psi^{\dagger}(0,0)\right\rangle_{0}=-\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathrm{sgn}(t)}{2\pi x_{c}}\left\langle T_{t}e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi(t,x)}e^{\mathrm{i}\phi(0,0)}\right\rangle_{0}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}(t+x)+x_{c}\mathrm{sgn}(t)}

\displaystyle G^{R}(t,x)=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\theta(t)\left\langle\{\psi(t,x),\psi^{\dagger}(0,0)\}\right\rangle_{0}\overset{T\to 0^{+}}{=}-\mathrm{i}\frac{\theta(t)}{2\pi}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}(t+x)+x_{c}}+\frac{1}{-\mathrm{i}(t+x)+x_{c}}\right)=-\mathrm{i}\theta(t)\cdot\delta_{x_{c}}(t+x)\ .

(70)

Note that time-ordering of fermion operators introduces a minus sign when two fermion operators are exchanged, whereas this is not the case for bosonic operators.
The overall signs (
-1
,
-\mathrm{i}
,
-\mathrm{i}
) for the imaginary-time, real-time, and retarded Green’s functions follow the standard conventions.

Appendix B
The quantum impurity model

B.1
The Anderson model

We consider an Anderson impurity problem with two spin flavors
s=\uparrow,\downarrow
and two “valley” flavors
l=\pm
.
The electron operators on the impurity are denoted as
f_{ls}
, and the electron operators in the effective one-dimensional chiral bath (see
appendix

A
for details) are denoted as
\psi_{ls}(x)
.
The
2\times 2
Pauli matrices regarding
l
and
s
will be dubbed as
[\sigma^{\mu}]_{l,l^{\prime}}
and
[\varsigma^{\nu}]_{s,s^{\prime}}
, respectively, with
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
.
In realistic systems such as magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (MATBG) (see
section

B.6
for details), this “valley” degree of freedom can either represent two degenerate momentum valleys, or represent two degenerate Wannier orbitals that carry opposite orbital angular momenta (OAM) and the corresponding partial waves in the bath.
For both cases,
l
will eventually become an internal degree of freedom (just as spin), despite that we still refer to it as a “valley” index.

We will assume the impurity model to respect the unitary symmetry group of
[{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{c}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}\times D_{\infty}]/\mathrm{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
Here
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{c}
is generated by
\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}
,
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
is generated by
\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{x,y,z}
, and
\mathrm{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}=\{\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0},-\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}\}
.

D_{\infty}={\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}\rtimes{\mathrm{Z}_{2}}
is the valley symmetry group, where the
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}
charge conservation is generated by
\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}
, while the
{\mathrm{Z}_{2}}
component is generated by an valley-flipping action dubbed as
C_{2}=\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0}
, which guarantees
l
and
\overline{l}
to remain degenerate.
Rotations in the dihedral group
D_{\infty}
follows the algebra relation
C_{2}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\varphi\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}}\cdot C_{2}=e^{-\mathrm{i}\varphi\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}}
.
Origins of these symmetries in the context of MATBG will be reviewed in
section

B.6
.

The Anderson model is

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=H_{0}+H_{\rm hyb}+H_{\rm imp}

(71)

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ls}\sum_{k}k~:d_{ls}^{\dagger}(k)d_{ls}(k):\qquad\qquad H_{\rm hyb}=\sqrt{{2\Delta_{0}}}\sum_{ls}\left(\psi_{ls}^{\dagger}(0)f_{ls}+h.c.\right)

\displaystyle H_{\rm imp}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{f}\hat{N}+U\frac{\hat{N}(\hat{N}-1)}{2}+H_{\rm AH}

Here,
d_{ls}(k)
form the effective chiral fermion bath reproducing the constant hybridization function
\Delta_{0}
, with a Fourier transformation to an auxiliary one-dimensional real space
\psi_{ls}(x)
defined in
eq.

11
.
The one-dimensional space is assumed to be of length
L
(
L\to\infty
), with the boundary condition of bath electrons chosen as
\psi(-\frac{L}{2})=\psi(\frac{L}{2})e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi P_{\rm bc}}
, so that the momentum
k\in\frac{2\pi}{L}(\mathbb{Z}-P_{\rm bc}/2)
.
The normal-ordering
:\cdots:
to the bath electrons is defined in
eq.

10
.
Due to the
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
and
D_{\infty}
symmetries, the Fermi velocities of all bath flavors are degenerate, and the hybridization must be proportional to
\sigma^{0}\tau^{0}
.

H_{\rm imp}
is the impurity Hamiltonian that only involves
f
electrons, and its eigenstates are summarized in
table

2
.

\hat{N}=\sum_{ls}f_{ls}^{\dagger}f_{ls}
counts the total electron number on the impurity.

\epsilon_{f}
and
U
denote the on-site potential and the Hubbard repulsion, respectively, while
H_{\rm AH}
contains all other symmetry-allowed terms that split the
N
-electron levels into multiplets.
Since bilinear terms other than
\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}
(Zeeman splittings) necessarily violate the symmetries, we only discuss quartic interactions in
H_{\rm AH}
.

To find the most general form of
H_{\rm AH}
, it suffices to 1) classify all bilinear operators
f_{ls}f_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
into irreducible representations (irreps) of the symmetry groups
D_{\infty}
and
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
, which we refer to as different scattering channels, and 2) assign independent scattering amplitudes to each channel.
Since a common scattering amplitude to all channels can be absorbed to a re-definition of Hubbard
U
, it is convenient to choose one reference channel, and keep track of the relative differences of other channels.

It suffices to label the irreps of
D_{\infty}
and
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
independently, as the two groups commute.
For the valley symmetry group
D_{\infty}
, we first define the following operator that counts the total
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}
charge,

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{z}=\sum_{ls,l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}_{ls}[\sigma^{z}]_{l,l^{\prime}}[\varsigma^{0}]_{s,s^{\prime}}f_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\qquad\qquad\mathrm{with~eigenvalues~}L^{z}\in\mathbb{Z}

(72)

Since
C_{2}=\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0}
anti-commutes with
\hat{L}^{z}
,
+L^{z}
and
-L^{z}
states must be degenerate if
L^{z}\not=0
. Such irreps must hence be two-fold degenerate.
If
L^{z}=0
, on the other hand, then the irrep is non-degenerate. Nevertheless, its
C_{2}
eigenvalue can still have two choices,
\pm 1
. We dub these two irreps as
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively, following the notation of general
D_{n}
groups.
We introduce the notation
L=A_{1},A_{2},1,2,3,\cdots
to uniquely label the irreps of the
D_{\infty}
group.
For irreps with
L=1,2,3,\cdots
, the two degenrate states will be labeled by
L^{z}=\pm L
.
For the direct product of two irreps of
D_{\infty}
, there are the following rules,

\displaystyle A_{1}\otimes A_{1}=A_{1}\quad\qquad A_{1}\otimes A_{2}=A_{2}\quad\qquad A_{1}\otimes L=L\quad\qquad A_{2}\otimes L=L

(73)

\displaystyle L\otimes L^{\prime}=|L-L^{\prime}|\oplus|L+L^{\prime}|\quad\qquad\qquad L\otimes L=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\oplus 2L

where
L\not=0,L^{\prime}\not=0
, and
L\not=L^{\prime}
.
Also, note that all irreps of
D_{\infty}
are real (hence self-conjugate), testified from the Frobenius-Schur indicator,
\mathrm{FSI}[L]=\int\mathrm{d}g\cdot\chi^{(L)}(g^{2})
, where
\chi^{(L)}(g)
is the character of group element
g
in the irrep
L
, and
\int\mathrm{d}g\cdot 1=1
is the group measure.
To be specific,
D_{\infty}
consists of two connected components,
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}
and
C_{2}\cdot{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}
.
For the non-degenerate irreps
L=A_{1}
and
A_{2}
,
\chi^{(L)}(g^{2})=1
for all
g\in D_{\infty}
, hence
\mathrm{FSI}[L]=1
.
For the two-fold degenerate irreps,
L=1,2,3,\cdots
,
\chi^{(L)}\left(\left(e^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{z}}\right)^{2}\right)=2\cos(L\theta)
, hence the
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}
component contributes zero to the FSI, while as
(C_{2}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{z}})^{2}=1
, hence
\chi^{(L)}\left(\left(C_{2}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{z}}\right)^{2}\right)=2
. After the integral,
\mathrm{FSI}[L]=1
as well.

For
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
, we follow the standard notation of SU(2) groups, and define

\displaystyle\hat{S}^{\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ls,l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}_{ls}[\sigma^{0}]_{l,l^{\prime}}[\varsigma^{\nu}]_{s,s^{\prime}}f_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\qquad\qquad(\nu=x,y,z)\qquad\qquad\mathrm{with~eigenvalues~}S^{\nu}\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}

(74)

We will also denote
\hat{\mathbf{S}}=(\hat{S}^{x},\hat{S}^{y},\hat{S}^{z})
.
The irreps of
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
are uniquely labeled by the spin quantum number
S\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{2}
, which is defined from the eigenvalues of
\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}=\sum_{\nu=x,y,z}(\hat{S}^{\nu})^{2}=S(S+1)
in the standard way.
The degeneracy of an irrep with spin-
S
is
2S+1
, where the degenerate states are distinguished by
S^{z}=-S,\cdots,S
.
The direct product of two irreps is given by
S\otimes S^{\prime}=|S-S^{\prime}|\oplus|S-S^{\prime}|+1\oplus\cdots\oplus|S+S^{\prime}|
.
All the irreps of
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
are also self-conjugate (real if
S
is integer, pseudo-real if half-integer).

The irreps of
[D_{\infty}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are hence labeled by
[L,S]
. All irreps are self-conjugate. The total degeneracy of irrep
[L,S]
is given by the product of the valley degeneracy with the spin degeneracy,

\mathrm{DEG}_{[L,S]}=\begin{cases}2S+1,\qquad&L=A_{1},A_{2}\\
4S+2,\qquad&L=1,2,3,\cdots\end{cases}

(75)

The valley degeneracy and spin degeneracy within each irrep, if non-trivial, will be labeled by
L^{z}
and
S^{z}
, respectively.

It is then direct to classify the bilinear operators
f_{ls}f_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
(a six-dimensional Hilbert space due to Pauli exclusion principle) into irreps.
To begin with,
f_{+\uparrow}f_{-\uparrow}
and its
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
rotations span a spin-triplet, and since
L=0
, there is no additional valley degeneracy.
We term this irrep as a triplet (‘
T
’,
[L,S]=[A_{2},1]
), according to its total degeneracy
3
.
Next, the remaining three states necessarily form spin-singlets, and according to their valley charge, can be further classified into an
L=2
doublet (‘
D
’,
[L,S]=[2,0]
) and an
L=0
singlet (‘
S
’,
[L,S]=[A_{1},0]
).
The wave-functions of these scattering channels are summarized in
table

2
, with expressions identical to those irreps of two-electron states.

We choose the triplet channel as the reference channel. Then the general form of the multiplet splitting reads

\displaystyle H_{\rm AH}

\displaystyle=-J_{S}\frac{f^{\dagger}_{+\uparrow}f^{\dagger}_{-\downarrow}-f^{\dagger}_{+\downarrow}f^{\dagger}_{-\uparrow}}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{f_{-\downarrow}f_{+\uparrow}-f_{-\uparrow}f_{+\downarrow}}{\sqrt{2}}-J_{D}\sum_{l=\pm}f^{\dagger}_{l\uparrow}f^{\dagger}_{l\downarrow}f_{l\downarrow}f_{l\uparrow}

(76)

\displaystyle=-\frac{J_{S}}{4}\sum_{ll^{\prime}s}f_{ls}^{\dagger}f_{\bar{l}\bar{s}}^{\dagger}f_{\bar{l}^{\prime}\bar{s}}f_{l^{\prime}s}-\frac{J_{D}}{2}\sum_{ls}f_{ls}^{\dagger}f_{l\bar{s}}^{\dagger}f_{l\bar{s}}f_{ls}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}\sum_{l_{1}l_{1}^{\prime}l_{2}l_{2}^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}_{l_{1}s}f^{\dagger}_{l_{1}^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}J_{D}&0&0&0\\
0&\frac{J_{S}}{2}&\frac{J_{S}}{2}&0\\
0&\frac{J_{S}}{2}&\frac{J_{S}}{2}&0\\
0&0&0&J_{D}\\
\end{pmatrix}_{l_{1}^{\prime}l_{1},l_{2}^{\prime}l_{2}}f_{l_{2}^{\prime}s^{\prime}}f_{l_{2}s}

with
J_{S}
and
J_{D}
being parameters to be determined.
In the 3rd line,
(l^{\prime}l)=(++),(+-),(-+),(--)
.
To see that the 3rd line equals the 2nd line, simply note that the
s=s^{\prime}
matrix elements in the 3rd line will be canceled after imposing fermion anti-parity. The
s=\overline{s^{\prime}}
elements recover the 2nd line. The 3rd line will be useful in
section

B.6
.

In this paper, we discuss the physics for general
J_{S,D}
that satisfy
|J_{S,D}|\ll U
.
If
J_{S}
or
J_{D}
is positive, the ground state(s) will be spin-singlet(s), hence we will term the splitting as of the anti-Hund’s type; while if both
J_{S}
and
J_{D}
are negative, we term it as of the Hund’s type.

It is also useful to re-organize
H_{\rm imp}
as

\displaystyle H_{\rm imp}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{f}\hat{N}+\left(U-\frac{1}{4}J_{S}\right)\frac{\hat{N}(\hat{N}-1)}{2}+J_{S}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}-\left(J_{D}-\frac{1}{4}J_{S}\right)\sum_{l}\hat{N}_{l\uparrow}\hat{N}_{l\downarrow}

(77)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{f}\hat{N}+\left(U-\frac{1}{4}J_{S}\right)\frac{\hat{N}(\hat{N}-1)}{2}+J_{S}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}-\left(J_{D}-\frac{1}{4}J_{S}\right)\frac{\hat{N}^{2}+(\hat{L}^{z})^{2}-2\hat{N}}{4}

Here,
\hat{S}^{\nu}_{l}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{ss^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}_{ls}[\varsigma^{\nu}]_{s,s^{\prime}}f_{ls^{\prime}}
is the spin operator in valley-
l
, and
\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{l}=(\hat{S}^{x}_{l},\hat{S}^{y}_{l},\hat{S}^{z}_{l})
.
\hat{N}_{ls}=f_{ls}^{\dagger}f_{ls}
so that
\hat{N}=\sum_{ls}\hat{N}_{ls}
.

In
table

2
, we tabulate all the eigenstates of
H_{\rm imp}
, according to the
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{c}
charge
N
, and the good quantum numbers
[L,S]
of
[D_{\infty}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Notice that, all the one-electron states are dictated to be degenerate, as they form the
[L,S]=[1,\frac{1}{2}]
irrep, and so are the three-electron states. Therefore, multiplet splitting only occurs in the two-electron subspace.
The eigen-energies can be directly read off from
eq.

77
. To begin with,
\hat{N}
and
(\hat{L}^{z})^{2}=L^{2}
are already given by good quantum numbers.
By writing
J_{S}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}=\frac{1}{2}J_{S}[\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}-\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}^{2}-\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{2}]
, where
\hat{\mathbf{S}}=\sum_{l=\pm}\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{l}
, we can also conveniently evaluate this term.
Concretely, except for the two-electron singlet and triplets, all other states have at least one
l
with
\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{l}^{2}=0
, therefore,
J_{S}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}
vanishes.
For both the singlet and triplets,
\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}^{2}=\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2}+1)
, while
\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}=0
for the singlet, and
\hat{\mathbf{S}}^{2}=1(1+1)
for the triplet.
Consequently,
J_{S}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}=-\frac{3}{4}J_{S}
for the the singlet, and
J_{S}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{+}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{S}}_{-}=\frac{1}{4}J_{S}
for the the triplet.
Adding up contribution from all terms leads to
table

2
.

[L,S]

\mathrm{DEG}_{[L,S]}

energy of
H_{\rm imp}

E_{0}=0

\forall~l,s

E_{1}=\epsilon_{f}

E_{S}=2\epsilon_{f}+U-J_{S}

[2,0]

Table 2:
Multiplet levels, diagonalized from the impurity Hamiltonian
H_{\rm imp}

eq.

77
.

Time-reversal and particle-hole symmetries

The model also commutes with an anti-unitary symmetry
C_{2}T
that acts as
(C_{2}T)f_{ls}(C_{2}T)^{-1}=f_{ls}
and
(C_{2}T)d_{ls}(k)(C_{2}T)^{-1}=d_{ls}(k)
.
It originates from the physical (Kramer’s spinful) time-reversal symmetry, in product with an
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
rotation and a
C_{2}
action, and is hence made “spinless” and “valley-less”.
Its origin in MATBG will also be discussed in
section

B.6
.
When represented in the auxiliary chiral bath, it does not reverse the momentum
k
, and hence when Fouriered to the auxiliary real-space, it will map
(C_{2}T)\psi_{ls}(x)(C_{2}T)^{-1}=\psi_{ls}(-x)
.
Despite this, we still refer to it as a time-reversal symmetry (TRS).

If
P_{\rm bc}=0
or
1
, we can also define a (unitary) charge conjugation
f_{ls}\to f_{ls}^{\dagger}
,
f_{ls}^{\dagger}\to f_{ls}
,
d_{ls}(k)\to-d_{ls}^{\dagger}(-k)
,
d_{ls}^{\dagger}(k)\to-d_{ls}(-k)
, which leaves
H_{0}+H_{\rm hyb}
invariant.
It transforms the operators contained in
H_{\rm imp}
as

\hat{N}\to 4-\hat{N},\qquad(\hat{S}_{l}^{x},\hat{S}_{l}^{y},\hat{S}_{l}^{z})\to(-\hat{S}_{l}^{x},\hat{S}_{l}^{y},-\hat{S}_{l}^{z})\qquad\hat{L}^{z}\to-\hat{L}^{z}\ .

(78)

Using these relations, we find that
H_{\rm imp}
is invariant under charge conjugation if
\epsilon_{f}
is tuned to the particle-hole symmetric point (PHS)

\epsilon_{f}=-\frac{3}{2}U+\frac{1}{4}J_{S}+\frac{1}{2}J_{D}\qquad\text{(PHS)}\ .

(79)

Fully anti-symmetrized form of the local interaction

For later convenience, we fully anti-symmetrize
H_{\rm imp}
as

H_{\rm imp}=\epsilon_{f}\hat{N}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{1234}\Gamma^{0}_{1234}f_{1}^{\dagger}f_{2}^{\dagger}f_{3}f_{4}

(80)

where the Arabic numbers are composite indices, i.e.,
1\equiv(l_{1},s_{1})
,
2\equiv(l_{2},s_{2})
,
etc.
.
We can read the (not fully anti-symmetrized yet) vertex function from
eq.

77
(more concretely, the
U
term can be re-written as
\frac{U}{2}\hat{N}(\hat{N}-1)=\frac{U}{2}\sum_{12}f^{\dagger}_{1}f^{\dagger}_{2}f_{2}f_{1}
, while the
J_{S}
and
J_{D}
terms can be more conveniently read from
eq.

76
) as

2U\cdot\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}-2J_{D}\cdot\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{3}l_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}-J_{S}\cdot\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{3}\bar{l}_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}

(81)

The fully anti-symmetrized vertex is given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}_{1234}=

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}_{U}\cdot\left(\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}-\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\right)+\Gamma^{0}_{D}\cdot\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{3}l_{4}}\left(\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}-\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\Gamma^{0}_{S}}{2}\cdot\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{3}\bar{l}_{4}}\left(\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}-\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\right)

(82)

The bare parameters are given by
\Gamma^{0}_{U}=U
,
\Gamma^{0}_{D}=J_{D}
,
\Gamma^{0}_{S}=J_{S}
.
These parameters may flow under renormalization, but the form of
\Gamma^{0}
will remain unchanged, as it is already the most general form allowed by the symmetry group.

eq.

71
with
H_{\rm imp}
given by
eq.

77
(with general
J_{S}
and
J_{D}
) defines the impurity problem.
However, there are several special limits of
J_{S},J_{D}
, where the symmetry group
{\mathrm{U}(2)}_{c,s}\times D_{\infty}
is further enlarged.

The
\rm U(4)
limit

When
J_{D}=J_{S}=0
, the Anderson model is fully
\rm U(4)
symmetric, with generators given by
\sigma^{\mu}\varsigma^{\nu}
for
\mu,\nu=0,x,y,z
.
No multiplet splitting is allowed to occur.
Accordingly, in the fully anti-symmetric vertex, only
\Gamma^{0}_{U}
survives, while
\Gamma^{0}_{S}=\Gamma^{0}_{D}
remains 0.

The
[{\rm U(2)}_{c,s}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{v}]\ /\ \mathbb{Z}_{2}
limit

Here
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0},-\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}\}
.
When
J_{D}=J_{S}\neq 0
, the doublet and singlet become degenerate, and the valley symmetry group will be promoted to an
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{v}
group, generated by
\sigma^{x,y,z}\varsigma^{0}
.
In particular, the original
C_{2}=\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0}
action can be understood as
e^{-\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}}\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\frac{\pi}{2}\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0}}
, a product of a
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{c}
rotation and an
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{v}
rotation.

We now derive the vertex function in this limit.
We denote
\Gamma^{0}_{J}\equiv\Gamma^{0}_{S}=\Gamma^{0}_{D}
and split
\Gamma_{U}^{0}=(\Gamma_{U}^{0}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{J}^{0})+\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{J}^{0}
.
Then the vertex function can be written as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}_{1234}=\left(\Gamma^{0}_{U}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{0}_{J}\right)\cdot\left(\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}-\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\Gamma^{0}_{J}}{2}\cdot\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\left(\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}-\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{3}\bar{l}_{4}}-2\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\right)-\frac{\Gamma^{0}_{J}}{2}\cdot\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}\left(\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}-\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{3}\bar{l}_{4}}-2\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\right)

(83)

To simplify the first term in the second row, we rewrite

\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}=\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}+\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}=\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}+\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{3}\bar{l}_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}
.
Then, using
1-\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}=\delta_{l_{2}\bar{l}_{3}}
, the Kronecker delta functions involving
l
-indices become

-\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}-\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{3}\bar{l}_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}\bar{l}_{3}}=-\delta_{l_{1}l_{2}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}-\delta_{l_{1}\bar{l}_{2}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}=-\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}
.
Hence, the first term in the second row is proportional to

-\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}
.
The second term in the second row is obtained by permuting the indices 3 and 4.
Therefore, the vertex equals to

\displaystyle\Gamma^{0}_{1234}=

\displaystyle\left(\Gamma^{0}_{U}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{0}_{J}\right)\cdot\left(\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}-\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\right)+\frac{\Gamma^{0}_{J}}{2}\cdot\left(\delta_{l_{1}l_{4}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{3}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{3}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{4}}-\delta_{l_{1}l_{3}}\delta_{l_{2}l_{4}}\delta_{s_{1}s_{4}}\delta_{s_{2}s_{3}}\right)\ ,

(84)

which has the form of the models in Refs.
Yoshimori 1976
;
Nishikawa et al. 2010
.
Comparing the above equation to Eq. (4.1) of Ref.
Yoshimori 1976
, we identify our
\Gamma^{0}_{U}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{0}_{J}
and
\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{J}^{0}
as
\Gamma_{C}
and
-\Gamma_{e}
of Ref.
Yoshimori 1976
, respectively.

The
\rm U(2)_{+}\times U(2)_{-}\rtimes Z_{2}
limit

When
J_{S}=0
, the spins in the
l=\pm
valleys are conserved independently.
Since
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{c}
and
{\mathrm{U}(1)}_{v}
are also preserved, the charges in the
l=\pm
valleys are conserved independently as well.
We dub the continuous group generated by
\frac{\sigma^{0}+l\cdot\sigma^{z}}{2}\varsigma^{0,x,y,z}
as
{\rm U(2)}_{l}
for
l=\pm
, which is the charge-spin rotation group per valley-
l
.
Note the valley-flipping
\rm Z_{2}
factor (generated by
\sigma_{x}
) is not promoted to a continuous symmetry in this case.
We can use the valley quantum number
L
and two spin quantum numbers
S_{l}
for
l=\pm
to label the irreps of scattering channels or two-electron states.
The doublet states (
[L,S]=[2,0]
) are now denoted as
[L,S_{+},S_{-}]=[2,0,0]
.
The singlet (
[L,S]=[0,0]
) and triplet (
[L,S]=[0,1]
) states now become degenerate, as they can be related by an independent spin rotation in
l=+
and/or
l=-
. They together form a four-fold degenerate irrep
[L,S_{+},S_{-}]=[0,\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
.

B.2
The Kondo model

irrep
[L,S]

\mathrm{DEG}_{[L,S]}

basis

[A_{1},0]

1

\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}

[A_{2},0]

1

\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}

[2,0]

2

\sigma^{x,y}\varsigma^{0}

[A_{1},1]

3

\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{x,y,z}

[A_{2},1]

3

\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{x,y,z}

[2,1]

6

\sigma^{x,y}\varsigma^{x,y,z}

Table 3:
Hermitian bilinear bath operators classified into irreps of
[L,S]
.

We focus the parameter regime
\epsilon_{f}\approx-\frac{3}{2}U
, where the low-energy configurations are dominated by two-
f
-electron states, and the multiplet splitting plays a significant role.
To obtain the corresponding low-energy theory, we need to carry out a Schrieffer-Wolff (SW) transformation
e^{\mathrm{i}S}
to integrate out the charge fluctuations on the
f
-impurity that cost
\mathcal{O}(U)
energies, resulting in an effective Kondo model.
For later use, we sketch the formal procedures.

1) Organize the Hilbert space into the low-energy subspace, which contains exactly two
f
-electrons, and the high-energy subspace, which contains
0,1,3
or
4

f
-electrons.
The projectors to the two subspaces are denoted as
\mathbb{P}_{2}
and
1-\mathbb{P}_{2}=\mathbb{P}_{0}+\mathbb{P}_{1}+\mathbb{P}_{3}+\mathbb{P}_{4}
, respectively. The subscripts indicate the
f
-electron numbers.
We may further divide
\mathbb{P}_{2}=\mathbb{P}_{S}+\mathbb{P}_{D}+\mathbb{P}_{T}
, where
\mathbb{P}_{S}=|S\rangle\langle S|
,
\mathbb{P}_{D}=\sum_{L^{z}=2,\overline{2}}|D,L^{z}\rangle\langle D,L^{z}|
, and
\mathbb{P}_{T}=\sum_{S^{z}=1,0,\overline{1}}|T,S^{z}\rangle\langle T,S^{z}|
.

H_{0}+H_{\rm imp}
is already diagonal in the
\mathbb{P}_{2}
and
1-\mathbb{P}_{2}
subspaces, while
H_{\rm hyb}
induces off-diagonal elements between
\mathbb{P}_{2}
and
\mathbb{P}_{3}
and between
\mathbb{P}_{2}
and
\mathbb{P}_{1}
.
The energy “gap” between the two subspaces is
\mathcal{O}(U)
, while the off-diagonal elements are
\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{0})
.

2) To eliminate these off-diagonal elements (perturbatively, in powers of
\frac{\Delta_{0}}{U}
), we devise such a Hermitian operator
S=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}S^{(n)}
, where
S^{(n)}
is of order
\mathcal{O}\left((\frac{\Delta_{0}}{U})^{n}\right)
.
If further assuming that
U
is much larger than the bath electron band width, the leading order
S^{(1)}
takes the form of

\displaystyle S^{(1)}=\left(\sum_{\Gamma=S,D,T}A_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{3}\Big(\sum_{ls}f^{\dagger}_{ls}\psi_{ls}(0)\Big)\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma}+\sum_{\Gamma=S,D,T}B_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{1}\Big(\sum_{ls}\psi^{\dagger}_{ls}(0)f_{ls}\Big)\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma}\right)+\mathrm{H.c.}

(85)

where
A_{\Gamma},B_{\Gamma}
are of order
\mathcal{O}(\frac{\Delta_{0}}{U})
and to be determined.

e^{\mathrm{i}S}
serves as a slight unitary rotation between the low- and high-energy subspaces.

3) Compute
\widetilde{H}=e^{\mathrm{i}S}He^{-\mathrm{i}S}=H+[\mathrm{i}S,H]+\frac{1}{2}[\mathrm{i}S,[\mathrm{i}S,H]]+\cdots
and express each term using the
original

f
and
\psi
operators.
Unknown parameters in
S
are determined by requiring that off-diagonal elements vanish, namely,
(1-\mathbb{P}_{2})\widetilde{H}\mathbb{P}_{2}=0
.
At the leading order
\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{0})
, this implies that
(1-\mathbb{P}_{2})\left(H_{\rm hyb}+[\mathrm{i}S^{(1)},H_{0}+H_{\rm imp}]\right)\mathbb{P}_{2}=0
, which fixes
A_{\Gamma}
and
B_{\Gamma}
.
Here,
\mathbb{P}_{2}
is still defined according to the particle number of
f
-operators; however, after the gauge transformation, this
f
-operator does
not
annihilate a physical electron.
Instead, the physical electron operator reads
\widetilde{f}=e^{\mathrm{i}S}fe^{-\mathrm{i}S}=f+[\mathrm{i}S^{(1)},f]+\mathcal{O}((\frac{\Delta_{0}}{U})^{2})
.
We will discuss this aspect in more detail in
section

B.5
.

4) As
\widetilde{H}
is now diagonal in the
\mathbb{P}_{2}
and
1-\mathbb{P}_{2}
subspaces, we simply keep the low-energy one,
\mathbb{P}_{2}\widetilde{H}\mathbb{P}_{2}
.
At the leading
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Delta_{0}^{2}}{U}\right)
order, we obtain the Kondo Hamiltonian as
H_{\rm K}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\mathbb{P}_{2}[S^{(1)},H_{\rm hyb}]\mathbb{P}_{2}
.
In general,
H_{\rm K}
may contain a term that only acts on the impurity; however, this term can be absorbed as a slight shift to the multiplet energies,
E_{\Gamma}\to E_{\Gamma}+\delta E_{\Gamma}
, which are free parameters to begin with. We therefore neglect it.
Remaining terms in
H_{\rm K}
will be a coupling between a bilinear operator of bath electrons, and an impurity operator, namely, the Kondo coupling.

{\mathrm{U}(4)}
symmetric Kondo model

The SW transformation carried out for the
{\mathrm{U}(4)}
symmetric case can be found in previous work
Zhou et al. 2024
.
We review the result concisely here.
H_{\rm K}
contains an
{\mathrm{SU}(4)}
moment-moment interaction
\zeta
(anti-ferromagnetic,
\zeta>0
), and a density-density interaction
\gamma
,

\displaystyle H_{\rm K}~=~(2\pi\zeta)\cdot\sum_{\mu\nu\not=00}\Theta^{\mu\nu}\cdot\psi^{\dagger}\sigma^{\mu}\varsigma^{\nu}\psi~~+~~(2\pi\gamma)\cdot\mathbb{P}_{2}~\cdot~:\psi^{\dagger}\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}\psi:

(86)

Here, we have defined the representation of the
{\mathrm{SU}(4)}
generators on the 6 two-electron states as

\displaystyle\Theta^{\mu\nu}=\mathbb{P}_{2}\frac{f^{\dagger}\sigma^{\mu}\varsigma^{\nu}f}{2}\mathbb{P}_{2}\qquad\qquad\mu\nu\not=00

(87)

and abbreviated
\psi^{\dagger}\sigma^{\mu}\varsigma^{\nu}\psi=\sum_{ls,l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\psi^{\dagger}_{ls}(0)[\sigma^{\mu}]_{ll^{\prime}}[\varsigma^{\nu}]_{ss^{\prime}}\psi_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}(0)
.
If not specified, bath operators in this section all live at
x=0
.

\zeta
will grow under renormalization, and the system will flow to a Kondo Fermi liquid, where the impurity
{\mathrm{SU}(4)}
moment gets exactly screened by another
{\mathrm{SU}(4)}
moment in the bath.
Remaining bath electrons sees a
\frac{\pi}{2}
phase shift at the origin.

General Kondo couplings with
[{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}\times D_{\infty}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
C_{2}T
symmetries

We now show that, compared to
eq.

86
, the lower symmetry in the general impurity model simply leads to an “anisotropy” in the
{\mathrm{SU}(4)}
moment-moment couplings
\zeta
, characterized by 5 independent real-valued parameters, as well as allowing the density-density coupling to
\mathbb{P}_{S}
,
\mathbb{P}_{D}
, and
\mathbb{P}_{T}
manifolds to be independent.
No ‘new’ terms are additionally brought about.
The result is summarized in
table

4
.

For this sake, a symmetry analysis suffices.
As
H_{\rm K}
must be Hermitian, it suffices to separately check the Hermitian impurity operators and the Hermitian bilinear bath operators, and classify them into irreps labeled by
[L,S]
.
According to the discussions around
eq.

73
, if and only if the impurity operators and the bath operators span the same irrep, their tensor product contains an identity irrep (
[A_{1},0]
) that remains invariant under
[D_{\infty}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
Finally, imposing
C_{2}T
further rules out some choices.

For the Hermitian bilinear bath operators
\psi^{\dagger}_{ls}\psi_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
, which span a
4^{2}=16
dimensional Hilbert space, the decomposition is direct.
As both
\psi^{\dagger}_{ls}
and
\psi_{l^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
spans the
[1,\frac{1}{2}]
irrep (all the irreps of
[D_{\infty}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
are self-conjugate, so we do not need to distinguish the irreps of ‘bras’ from ‘kets’, see
section

B.1
), the valley part follows
1\otimes 1=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\oplus 2
(see
eq.

73
), while the spin part follows
\frac{1}{2}\otimes\frac{1}{2}=0\oplus 1
.
The basis operators spanning each irrep are tabulated in
table

3
.
Crucially, each irrep appears just for once.

For the impurity operators
|\Xi\rangle\langle\Xi^{\prime}|
, they span a
6^{2}=36
dimensional Hilbert space.
Both
|\Xi\rangle
and
\langle\Xi^{\prime}|
span a reducible representation
[A_{1},0]\oplus[2,0]\oplus[A_{2},1]
(also dubbed as ‘
S\oplus D\oplus T
’, see
table

2
).
To begin with, there are ‘irrep-diagonal’ operators.
For the ‘
S
’ manifold,
[A_{1},0]\otimes[A_{1},0]=[A_{1},0]
, and the operator is given by
\mathbb{P}_{S}
.
For the ‘
D
’ manifold,
[2,0]\otimes[2,0]=[A_{1},0]\oplus[A_{2},0]\oplus[4,0]
, where
[A_{1},0]
is given by
\mathbb{P}_{D}
, and
[A_{2},0]
is given by

\displaystyle\Theta^{z0}=|D,2\rangle\langle D,2|-|D,\overline{2}\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|

(88)

as defined in
eq.

87
.
The operators spanning
[4,0]
will be dubbed as

\displaystyle\Lambda_{x}=|D,2\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|+\mathrm{H.c.}\qquad\qquad\Lambda_{y}=-\mathrm{i}|D,2\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|+\mathrm{H.c.}

(89)

which do not belong to
eq.

87
, and cannot find the corresponding bath bilinear operators to enter the Kondo coupling.
For the ‘
T
’ manifold,
[A_{2},1]\otimes[A_{2},1]=[A_{1},0]\oplus[A_{1},1]\oplus[A_{1},2]
.
The
[A_{1},0]
irrep is given by
\mathbb{P}_{T}
, while
[A_{1},1]
is the spin-1 operators
\Theta^{0x,0y,0z}
in
eq.

87
.
The
[A_{1},2]
irrep does not belong to
eq.

87
, and does not appear in Kondo coupling as well.

Then, there are ‘irrep-off-diagonal’ operators. Let us take the off-diagonal blocks between
S
and
D
manifolds as an example.
Since there are two blocks that are Hermitian conjugate to each other,
|S\rangle\langle D,L^{z}|
and
|D,L^{z}\rangle\langle S|
, the irrep
[A_{1},0]\otimes[2,0]=[2,0]
appears twice.
We can Hermitize the basis for the two irreps as following. One is

\displaystyle\Theta^{x0}=\frac{|S\rangle\langle D,2|+|S\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|}{\sqrt{2}}+\mathrm{H.c.}\qquad\qquad\Theta^{y0}=\mathrm{i}\frac{|S\rangle\langle D,2|-|S\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|}{\sqrt{2}}+\mathrm{H.c.}

(90)

which follows the definition of
eq.

87
, and the other is

\displaystyle\Phi^{x0}=\mathrm{i}\frac{|S\rangle\langle D,2|+|S\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|}{\sqrt{2}}+\mathrm{H.c.}\qquad\qquad\Phi^{y0}=-\frac{|S\rangle\langle D,2|-|S\rangle\langle D,\overline{2}|}{\sqrt{2}}+\mathrm{H.c.}

(91)

which does not belong to
eq.

87
.
It can be directly verified that (as
{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}
actions are all trivial here, they are not listed)

\displaystyle e^{\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}}\cdot(\Theta^{x0},\Theta^{y0})\cdot e^{-\mathrm{i}\theta\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}}

\displaystyle=(\Theta^{x0},\Theta^{y0})\begin{pmatrix}\cos(2\theta)&\sin(2\theta)\\
-\sin(2\theta)&\cos(2\theta)\\
\end{pmatrix}\qquad C_{2}\cdot(\Theta^{x0},\Theta^{y0})\cdot C_{2}=(\Theta^{x0},-\Theta^{y0})

(92)

and
(\Phi^{x0},\Phi^{y0})
and the bath operators
(\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0},\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{0})
transform in the same way.
Therefore, the coupling of
(\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0},\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{0})
to
(\Theta^{x0},\Theta^{y0})
and
(\Phi^{x0},\Phi^{y0})
are both allowed by
[D_{\infty}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.
However, they transform differently under
C_{2}T
:
(C_{2}T)(\Theta^{x0},\Theta^{y0})(C_{2}T)^{-1}=(\Theta^{x0},-\Theta^{y0})
, while
(C_{2}T)(\Phi^{x0},\Phi^{y0})(C_{2}T)^{-1}=(-\Phi^{x0},\Phi^{y0})
, while the bath operator behaves as
(C_{2}T)(\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0},\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{0})(C_{2}T)^{-1}=(\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{0},-\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{0})
.
Therefore, the bath operator can only couple to
\Theta^{x0,y0}
.

The same analysis also applies to the other off-diagonal blocks.
Between
S
and
T
, there are two
[A_{1},0]\otimes[A_{2},1]=[A_{2},1]
irreps, while only the one spanned by
\Theta^{zx,zy,zz}
is allowed by
C_{2}T
to couple to the bath
(\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{x},\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{y},\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{z})
.
Between
D
and
T
, there are two
[2,0]\otimes[A_{2},1]=[2,1]
irreps, while only the one spanned by
\Theta^{xx,xy,xz,yx,yy,yz}
is allowed by
C_{2}T
to couple to the bath
(\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{x},\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{y},\sigma^{x}\varsigma^{z},\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{x},\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{y},\sigma^{y}\varsigma^{z})
.

In sum, compared to the
{\mathrm{U}(4)}
symmetric case, no new Kondo coupling terms are allowed to appear due to the
[D_{\infty}\times{\mathrm{SU}(2)}_{s}]/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
C_{2}T
symmetries.
There is only “anisotropy” arsing in the coupling constants, as summarized in
table

4
.
In particular, for the moment-moment couplings, since the
{\mathrm{SU}(4)}
breaking effect is a perturbation (
J_{S,D}\ll U
, hence
\zeta_{\mu\nu}\sim\frac{\Delta_{0}^{2}}{U+\mathcal{O}(J_{S,D})}
has the same sign as
\frac{\Delta_{0}^{2}}{U}
), we can also expect the signs of the coupling constants to follow the
{\mathrm{U}(4)}
symmetric case, being anti-ferromagnetic.

Finally, we remark that, at PHS,
:\psi^{\dagger}\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}\psi:
acquires a minus sign under charge conjugation, yet
\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma}
(
\Gamma=S,D,T
) does not.
Therefore, the density-density coupling will be forbidden so that all
\gamma_{\Gamma}=0
.
Since the density-density coupling is in general not relevant under RG, we will take the advantage of assuming a PHS to ignore it.

Further down-folding the Kondo model

Suppose we are carrying out an RG (for example, a poorman scaling) to this general Kondo model with
H_{0}+H_{\rm imp}+H_{\rm K}
, where
H_{\rm imp}=\sum_{\Gamma=S,D,T}E_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\Gamma}
(see
table

2
), and an anti-ferromagnetic
H_{\rm K}
given by
table

4
.
As the charge fluctuation has been integrated out, we are starting with an initial energy scale
D
satisfying
|J_{S,D}|\ll D\ll U
.
Therefore, initially, the multiplet splitting induced by
J_{S,D}
is not important, and the five independent moment-moment couplings will remain approximately equal as
\zeta
.

\zeta
will grow as
D
is lowered, similar to the
{\mathrm{U}(4)}
symmetric case.
If
\zeta
already diverges at some
D_{\rm K}\gg|J_{S,D}|
(or equivalently speaking, the system flows to a strong-coupling fixed point, evidenced by
e.g.
the low-energy bath phase shift saturating
\frac{\pi}{2}
), then the system should share the same universal properties as the
{\mathrm{U}(4)}
symmetric model.
However, if
\zeta
has not diverged when
D
reaches the scale of multiplet splitting, yet we are still interested in physics with temperature
k_{B}T\ll D\sim|J_{S,D}|
, then we will have to further down-fold the low-energy Hilbert space.

In
sections

B.3
and
B.4
, we will discuss the further down-folded models in more detail.

irrep

\mathrm{DEG}_{[L,S]}

[A_{1},0]

\mathbb{P}_{S}

\gamma_{S}

Within
D

[A_{1},0]

1

\mathbb{P}_{D}

\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}

\gamma_{D}

[A_{2},0]

1

\Theta^{z0}

\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{0}

\lambda_{z}

[4,0]

2

\Lambda_{x,y}

-

-

Within
T

[A_{1},0]

1

\mathbb{P}_{T}

\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{0}

\gamma_{T}

[A_{1},1]

3

\Theta^{0x,0y,0z}

\sigma^{0}\varsigma^{x,y,z}

\zeta_{0z}

[A_{1},2]

5

|T,1\rangle\langle T,\overline{1}|
, etc

-

-

Between
S,D

[2,0]

\zeta_{x}

Between
S,T

[A_{2},1]

3

\sigma^{z}\varsigma^{x,y,z}

[2,1]
